=== BUGGY CODE ===
class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        mem=defaultdict(int)
        def dfs(i,val=0):
            if i=len(s): 
                return 1
            if (i,val in mem:
                return mem[i,val]
            p=0
            if s[i]=="D:
                for ind in range(0,val+2): 
                    p+=dfs(i+1,ind)%(10**9+7)
            else:
                if ind in range(val+1,i+3): 
                    p+=dfs(i+1,ind)%(10**9+7)
            mem[i,val]=p
            return p
        return dfs(0)

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;
- moving from a local repair into a fresh branch of speculative causes when the current path has not been rechecked yet;
- turning a focused fix into a broad rewrite unless the surrounding code clearly forces it.

Potential improved code:

```
from collections import defaultdict

class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        mem = defaultdict(int)

        def dfs(i: int, val: int) -> int:
            if i == len(s):
                return 1
            if (i, val) in mem:
                return mem[(i, val)]

            total = 0
            if s[i] == 'D':
                for nxt in range(val + 1):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD
            else:
                for nxt in range(val + 1, i + 2):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD

            mem[(i, val)] = total
            return total

        return dfs(0, 0)
```

At state i=1, length=2, adding third element ranks 0..2. If val=1, I next rank can be 2 only. range(2,3) -> 2. If val=0, next rank can be 1,2. range(1,3) -> 1,2. Good. i=2: length=3, adding fourth ranks 0..3; I range(val+1,4) -> max 3. Good. So upper bound i+2.

```
class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        mem = {}
        def dfs(i, val):
            if i == len(s):
                return 1
            if (i, val) in mem:
                return mem[(i, val)]
            total = 0
            if s[i] == 'D':
                for nxt in range(val + 1):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD
            else:
                for nxt in range(val + 1, i + 2):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD
            mem[(i, val)] = total
            return total
        return dfs(0, 0)
```

```
class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        memo = {}

        def dfs(i: int, val: int) -> int:
            if i == len(s):
                return 1
            if (i, val) in memo:
                return memo[(i, val)]

            total = 0
            if s[i] == 'D':
                for nxt in range(val + 1):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD
            else:
                for nxt in range(val + 1, i + 2):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD

            memo[(i, val)] = total
            return total

        return dfs(0, 0)
```

```
class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        memo = {}

        def dfs(i, val):
            if i == len(s):
                return 1
            if (i, val) in memo:
                return memo[(i, val)]

            total = 0
            if s[i] == 'D':
                for nxt in range(val + 1):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD
            else:
                for nxt in range(val + 1, i + 2):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD

            memo[(i, val)] = total
            return total

        return dfs(0, 0)
```

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in using relative rank DP for all D/I sequences: The recurrence with appending new elements and choosing rank j works because the relative order of old elements remains the same, but when new element is inserted, the ranks of old elements shift. We accounted for old last rank shift? Let's verify recurrence more rigorously.

At state i, we have a valid relative permutation of length i+1. Let last element L have rank val among these i+1 (0-based). We append a new element X. We want to know the rank j of X among the i+2 elements after insertion. The old elements' ranks may shift if X is inserted before them. The relative order of old elements remains unchanged. The new rank j can be any 0..i+1. We need condition based on comparison L vs X.

If X > L, then L's rank among new set is same val (because X is after L). X's rank j must be > val. Conversely, if X < L, then L's rank among new set is val+1 (because X before L). X's rank j must be <= val? Let's check if X's rank j can equal val+1? If X < L, then X is before L, so X's rank is at most val (since L's new rank is val+1, X is before L, so rank <= val). Thus j <= val. Good. So recurrence exact. Good.

- If s[i]=='D': val+1
- If s[i]=='I': i+1-val
Worst-case if all I: sum_{i=0}^{n} sum_{val=0}^{i} (i+1-val) = sum_{i=0}^{n} (i+1)(i+2)/2 ~ n^3/6. For n=200, ~1.35 million. Each transition is a recursive call, but many states are memoized; still 1.35M calls. Python recursion overhead 1.35M maybe okay (<1s? maybe 2s). LeetCode Python might be okay. If n=2000, not. But n=200. Good.

Final code:

```
class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        memo = {}

        def dfs(i, val):
            if i == len(s):
                return 1
            if (i, val) in memo:
                return memo[(i, val)]

            total = 0
            if s[i] == 'D':
                for nxt in range(val + 1):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD
            else:
                for nxt in range(val + 1, i + 2):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD

            memo[(i, val)] = total
            return total

        return dfs(0, 0)
```

dp0 [1]
I: dp1 [0,1]
D: dp2[j]=sum_{k=j}^{1} dp1[k]
j=0:1, j=1:1, j=2:0 -> [1,1,0]
I: dp3[j]=sum_{k=0}^{j-1} dp2[k]
j=0:0
j=1:1
j=2:2
j=3:2
Sum=5. Symmetric. Good.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        memo = {}

        def dfs(i, val):
            if i == len(s):
                return 1
            if (i, val) in memo:
                return memo[(i, val)]

            total = 0
            if s[i] == 'D':
                for nxt in range(val + 1):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD
            else:
                for nxt in range(val + 1, i + 2):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD

            memo[(i, val)] = total
            return total

        return dfs(0, 0)
